Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has been a critical paradigm for improving large language model (LLM) reasoning and agentic capabilities, but its efficiency remains a major bottleneck. Existing RL systems commonly rely on asynchronous execution to overlap rollout generation with policy optimization for enhanced efficiency. However, this approach faces inherent limitations: mild policy staleness offers only limited training efficiency gains, whereas aggressive asynchrony can degrade model quality and yields diminishing efficiency gains. The dynamic nature of LLM RL and multi-turn agentic workloads further challenges RL system design, as resource demands shift over time, making static resource allocation and generation strategy inefficient.
  This paper presents ReFlow, a system-algorithm co-designed RL framework for efficient LLM post-training. Its key insight is that, unlike existing RL frameworks which consume each trajectory only once, experience replay of recent trajectories enables effective sample reuse, thereby amortizing the high cost of rollout generation without incurring excessive policy staleness. To fully exploit this opportunity, ReFlow adopts a decoupled and adaptive architecture with three key designs: a dual replay data cycle supports efficient experience reuse while balancing training throughput and optimization stability; an elastic resource pool enables dynamic resource placement to accommodate evolving RL workloads; an adaptive scheduler coordinates generation strategies and resource allocation to improve resource utilization and boost generation throughput. Experiments across representative single-turn reasoning and multi-turn agentic RL tasks show that ReFlow improves training throughput by up to $2.06\times$ over state-of-the-art synchronous and asynchronous RL systems, while maintaining comparable or better model performance.

Second, over-asking has two mechanistic signatures: in four families the model demonstrably knows the request is unambiguous (the belief is decodable, causally inert and orthogonal to the decision) and asks anyway; in the fifth, the rare over-asks track different world-representations. Third, steering gated on a probe readout removes most of the simulated oversight burden while legitimate questions survive (bootstrap 95% intervals exclude zero); a prompt instruction makes it worse. We evaluate on a renewable variant of the IEEE 118-bus benchmark grid in which 26 of the conventional (synchronous) generators are replaced by inverter-based renewables with battery flexibility and standardised reactive-power limits (IEEE 1547), giving $97\,529$ physically valid (AC-OPF-feasible) operating scenarios, and we ablate all eight combinations of the transfer terms, reporting accuracy against ground truth and fidelity to the teacher separately. Distillation lowers student error on all four predicted quantities, by up to $48\%$ on active-power dispatch, and every distilled student is more accurate than its teacher on reactive-power dispatch. The gain is largely carried by the output matching term. The physics-residual term, in the layer-aggregated form used here, is consistently harmful, which we attribute to the spatial averaging it applies to a bus-level residual field. The resulting student is $32.5\times$ smaller in parameter count and has half the message-passing depth, making it a candidate for subsequent edge-device benchmarking.

Abstract: Risk-sensitive reinforcement learning (RL) addresses decision-making under uncertainty and adverse outcomes. Learning risk-sensitive policies from fixed datasets is important when additional interaction with the environment is costly. Existing work on risk-sensitive RL focuses on online settings or specific risk measures, leaving a general theoretical understanding of its offline setting underdeveloped. In this work, we investigate efficient offline risk-sensitive RL using the optimized certainty equivalent (OCE), a general class of risk measures. We consider two formulations: dynamic-OCE RL, which applies OCE recursively at each step, and static-OCE RL, which applies a single OCE to the cumulative rewards. Under the tabular setting, we develop pessimistic value-iteration algorithms for both formulations and establish tight sublinear suboptimality bounds, explicitly capturing how risk sensitivity, encoded by utility functions, affects learning difficulty. Moreover, we establish minimax suboptimality lower bounds to characterize the problem's fundamental limits. To handle large state spaces, we further develop pessimistic least-squares value-iteration algorithms that incorporate the general OCE risk criterion into linear function approximation. We establish utility-dependent sublinear guarantees with explicit dependence on the feature dimension $d$. To our knowledge, we provide the first offline algorithms along with the first upper and lower suboptimality bounds for both dynamic- and static-OCE RL.

Abstract: Many modern learning systems are used not to select a single best action, but to sample diverse high-quality outputs. Examples include entropy-regularized reinforcement learning, RLHF, and reward-guided generative modeling. Motivated by these settings, we study tilted sampling: an online learning problem in which the goal is to sample from the Gibbs distribution induced by an unknown reward function. We define sampling regret as the temperature-normalized cumulative KL divergence between the learner's distribution and the Gibbs target, corresponding to the suboptimality gap in entropy-regularized reward maximization. Our framework generalizes recent sampling-based and KL-regularized formulations in bandits and reinforcement learning. Our main contribution is a general regret framework that captures the different uncertainty structure of tilted sampling and classical regret minimization. While standard regret scales as $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{T})$ due to first-order uncertainty, sampling regret is governed by squared confidence widths, yielding logarithmic-in-$T$ regret in standard settings at fixed temperature. We develop sampling analogues of UCB and Thompson sampling (UCS and TS$^2$) and provide unified regret decompositions that explain and extend recent logarithmic guarantees, including the first Bayesian sampling-regret decomposition based on new bounds for posterior-random Gibbs distributions. Our results show that learning to sample from a soft-optimal distribution is statistically distinct from learning to identify a hard optimum.

Abstract: Knowing the layout of the physical world matters for many applications, yet the sensors we rely on for it (e.g., cameras and LiDAR) can fail in darkness, in adverse weather, or behind walls. Wireless signals do not face these limitations: every link between a base station and a user is shaped by the buildings it passes, reflects from, or is blocked by, leaving information about the surrounding scene embedded in the wireless channel measured at the endpoints. However, mapping channel measurements to the environment is a severely ill-posed problem: a measurement records only the structures that interact with the transmitted electromagnetic waves along its propagation paths, and even these observations can often be explained by multiple plausible scenes. We therefore formulate wireless scene reconstruction as posterior inference and develop a hierarchical framework with two complementary stages to approach this problem. A wireless foundation model, pretrained to respect propagation physics, transforms each channel measurement into a representation of the scene structure that shaped the observation. A diffusion model then jointly integrates representations from multiple measurements to model and sample the posterior over building occupancy, with consensus over samples producing the final reconstruction. We demonstrate that the framework can accurately recover building footprints from wireless channel measurements alone and show that its performance depends critically on both design components: physics-grounded representations expose scene-relevant information, while joint posterior inference coherently integrates complementary observations.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning agents optimize exactly the reward signal they are given, which can diverge sharply from an intended true objective when that signal is only a proxy for it; this failure mode is known as specification gaming. Existing evidence for this failure mode is largely observational, with comparatively few demonstrations pairing a controlled, deliberately constructed proxy/true gap with a mitigation that carries a verifiable guarantee rather than only an empirical improvement. We construct a minimal gridworld in which a cleaning-robot agent can either genuinely remove dirt or conceal it from a cheap sensor, and show that a standard deep Q-network trained only on the sensor's proxy reward converges to a policy that games it completely, reaching near-maximal proxy reward while never achieving the true objective. We then introduce a constrained reinforcement learning fix, penalizing the specific behaviors through which the agent exploits the gap, and show that it eliminates the gaming behavior from the final policy while substantially recovering true-objective performance. We further derive and empirically verify an exact accounting identity relating the proxy reward, true reward, and our cost signal for this environment, showing that the cost signal measures the proxy/true gap exactly rather than merely tracking it, and yields a deterministic guarantee under a hard-constrained variant of the method.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a widely used approach to post-training large language models. In a typical implementation, learning is off-policy: The policy generating the responses differs from the policy being trained because of training-inference mismatch or policy staleness in asynchronous training. This discrepancy is commonly addressed by importance sampling, whose ratio is usually regularized in practice through truncation, masking, or clipping. Under regularized importance sampling, the importance-weighted policy gradient is no longer invariant to constant shifts in the reward. This invariance is recovered by using the covariance between the reward and the importance-weighted score as the estimand, which coincides with the policy gradient when the ratio is not regularized. This note develops this covariance perspective for both sequence-level and token-level importance sampling. The covariance perspective reveals the roles of the group-mean baseline and score centering in policy gradient estimation. Both perform centering in covariance estimation, but they differ in the mean being subtracted: The group-mean baseline uses a sample mean, whereas score centering uses a population mean. The two lead to distinct unbiased covariance estimators with different variances. Whether both forms of centering should be used simultaneously depends on whether importance sampling is applied at the sequence level or at the token level. With sequence-level importance sampling, score centering adds no benefit once the group-mean baseline is used; with token-level importance sampling, using the group-mean baseline without score centering generally introduces bias when the importance ratio is regularized.

</details>

Abstract: Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) acquire new skills through fine-tuning, but it can destroy capabilities they already have: catastrophic forgetting remains a central obstacle to continual learning in LLMs. Recent works connect forgetting to how far, and along which directions, fine-tuning moves the weights. On-policy methods such as reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation avoid updating the principal singular directions of weight matrices and thus forget less. In contrast, SFT concentrates updates along these directions and is susceptible to catastrophic forgetting. In this work, we introduce Subspace Tuning (SST), which restricts each weight update to the bottom singular subspace, eliminating changes along the principal directions. SST is optimizer-agnostic and composes well with fine-tuning recipes including SFT and self-distillation. SST combines tight subspace protection, dense gradient estimation, and a simple refresh mechanism, enabling stronger learning than prior orthogonal fine-tuning methods. With these mechanisms, protecting as much as 99\% of the weight subspace substantially improves retention while preserving strong target-task performance. This behavior strengthens with model and data size and nearly eliminates forgetting in the large-model regime. SST also keeps the model close to its base distribution, inducing $2$--$4\times$ less KL drift than the baseline recipes. Overall, SST advances the learning--forgetting Pareto frontier, making it a practical default for fine-tuning pre-trained language models.

</details>

Abstract: We develop a robust reinforcement learning (RL) framework for learning adaptive treatment policies that generalize across patients and remain reliable under errors in the measured biomarker and shifts in the disease dynamics. Abstract: Time series foundation models are becoming increasingly popular for general-purpose forecasting. However, most leading models predict marginal quantiles, leaving the dependence structure of future trajectories unspecified. This distinction matters for trajectory-dependent quantities such as cumulative demand, peak load, and event timing, yet point and marginal scores do not reveal it. To model this dependence, we introduce PathFM, a trajectory-based forecasting model that uses flow matching to generate complete future paths. We further distill the model to one or two steps and share history attention cache across draws. Together, these enable PathFM to generate multiple paths for a single series in roughly the time needed for one query to a quantile model. The distilled sampler also makes it practical to optimize a calibration loss directly. PathFM achieves competitive marginal accuracy on the established forecasting benchmarks fev-bench and TIME. To complement these evaluations, we introduce Forecast Path Score Bench (fps-bench), which augments fev-bench tasks with energy and variogram scores and downstream metrics. PathFM achieves a higher aggregate path score on fps-bench than all evaluated external baselines. Experiments on chaotic systems further show that our flow model sustains long-run variability, while strong baselines rapidly collapse. PathFM makes generative time series forecasting both competitive and practical, enabling diverse downstream applications.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models are increasingly used for driving-scene understanding, yet their defensive reasoning under weakly structured Global South traffic remains unclear. We introduce BD-HazardVLM, a diagnostic benchmark of 500 Bangladesh road-scene images, comprising 375 from RSUD20K and 125 from TFP-BD, annotated for hazard presence, primary hazard category, risk timing, severity, defensive actions, visible evidence, and hallucination- and Western-road-bias traps. We evaluate Qwen2.5-VL-7B, InternVL3-8B, and MiniCPM-V-4.6 using a shared zero-shot task definition and structured output schema. Binary hazard-presence accuracy ranges from 88.0 percent to 89.2 percent, essentially matching the 89.0 percent majority-class baseline. Fine-grained reasoning is substantially weaker: category accuracy remains below 40 percent, risk-timing accuracy ranges from 1.6 percent to 12.4 percent, and exact defensive-action matching remains below 2 percent. Although InternVL3-8B achieves the highest severity accuracy at 61.2 percent, it has zero recall on the 58 high-severity cases. Qwen exhibits severe category mode collapse, while all models show weak latent-hazard anticipation and near-zero self-reported trap recognition. For the evaluated models, these findings show that coarse hazard recognition does not imply reliable fine-grained defensive-driving reasoning and motivate geographically diverse safety evaluation.

Abstract: Video generators serve as interactive worlds for exploration and simulation, yet realistic and visually appealing frames do not necessarily depict a consistent 3D world. Existing benchmarks assess selected geometric properties or use vision-language models to judge memory and consistency, leaving systematic 3D evaluation underexplored. We introduce Orbia, the first benchmark dedicated to a systematic and comprehensive evaluation of 3D consistency in interactive world models. Through controlled exploration and revisit, we organize the evaluation into three dimensions: input preservation, generated-content persistence, and 3D self-consistency. For input preservation, registered RGB-D references and annotated anchors support appearance, geometry, and spatial stability measurements. For generated-content persistence, we compare revisits to regions absent from the input image with their first generated observations to test whether models preserve what they create. For 3D self-consistency, cross-view depth and feature correspondence, regional scale agreement, and held-out Gaussian rendering test whether generated views describe the same 3D scene. Minute-long exploration and revisits further test long-term consistency. Our construction and annotation pipeline brings together public datasets, self-captured scenes, and simulation, yielding 720 instances across indoor and outdoor environments and six exploration-and-revisit protocols. Evaluation spans 27 text-, action-, and camera-conditioned models through native-interface adapters. Our Orbia benchmark provides a systematic basis for diagnosing 3D inconsistency and developing more persistent video world models.

</details>

Abstract: Image-to-text (I2T) understanding and text-to-image (T2I) generation are dual tasks in unified multimodal models (UMMs). Reconstructive post-training couples these tasks, but existing reconstruction-based methods remain perception-centric and provide limited supervision for reasoning about structure, intent, and transformation. We propose ReasonUni, a reasoning-augmented reconstructive post-training framework that extends this coupling beyond perception. Given an original image and an editing instruction, the UMM first reasons about what and how to edit and describes the target state, then generates the corresponding target image conditioned on this understanding output. To jointly optimize both modules, we introduce TreeGRPO, a tree-structured reinforcement learning method that samples multiple reasoning paths and candidate images per path, then aggregates visual rewards to update both modules under a unified objective. Experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate consistent gains in both understanding and generation, showing that reasoning-augmented reconstruction yields genuine mutual gains for UMMs.

</details>

Abstract: Recent reinforcement learning approaches have improved visual text generation, yet generated images still suffer from incorrect wording and malformed glyphs. We identify two coupled limitations: recognition-based rewards often overlook visible glyph defects, while whole-image scalar feedback obscures the uneven quality of individual text regions. These limitations motivate our central principle: $\textit{Blame errant words, not the canvas}$, to effectively preserve local quality distinctions. First, we introduce $\mathbf{TextDART}$, a defect-aware region-targeted reward model that localizes individual text regions and independently evaluates their semantic correctness and glyph quality, providing fine-grained, region-precise feedback. Building on TextDART, we propose $\mathbf{SCANNER}$, which turns these regional judgments into spatially differentiated policy feedback. It normalizes rewards for each text target across sampled images and casts the resulting regional advantages onto the corresponding latent tokens. Extensive experiments on existing benchmarks and our newly established $\textbf{PolyText-Bench}$ demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance in text accuracy and glyph fidelity. Project page: https://sujiehu.github.io/textdart-scanner

Abstract: Unified multimodal generation covers discrete, continuous, and hybrid paradigms. Discrete approaches with single-codebook visual tokenizers align naturally with text modeling but have limited representational capacity. Continuous and hybrid approaches retain richer visual representations, but they model text and images in heterogeneous spaces with different training objectives, potentially introducing optimization conflicts between modalities with misaligned representation spaces. These choices expose a central question: how to efficiently model high-capacity visual representations alongside textual content in a shared optimization space within a single backbone. We present Intern Lumina U2, to our knowledge the first diffusion language model to represent multi-codebook visual tokens in a fully discrete space. Each visual position is represented by a group of codes from complementary codebooks, increasing its representational capacity while preserving compatibility with discrete text modeling. At the input, the embeddings are concatenated at each position; at the output, a lightweight multi-codebook autoregressive head predicts the codes within each position sequentially, while the diffusion backbone processes all positions in parallel. Built on the unified 4D representation of AToken, Intern Lumina U2 supports image, video, and 3D inputs within a single backbone and achieves leading performance among unified models in visual understanding, image generation, and image editing. We further build a full-stack training, inference, and evaluation system on both Ascend and NVIDIA platforms to verify the cross-platform effectiveness of our model. We also improve the inference efficiency through a stability-aware sampling strategy, which combines confidence-plateau-guided unmasking approach with drift-triggered cache refreshing mechanisms, accelerating image generation by 4.45x.

</details>

Abstract: Map generation tasks feature extensive non-structural vectorized data (e.g., points, polylines, and polygons) and thus pose significant challenges to common pixel-wise generative models. Conventional approaches use multiple stages, first segmenting these features at the pixel level and then performing vectorized post-processing, with errors and complexity compounding at each stage. Motivated by the recent success of auto-regressive language modeling, we propose the first map foundation model, named Map Auto-Regressor (MARS), that is capable of generating both multi-polyline road networks and polygon buildings in a unified manner. For training MARS, we collected to our knowledge the largest multi-class map extraction dataset totaling 3.4M examples, which we call MAP-3M. Across four road and building datasets, MARS outperforms or matches the performance of multistage baselines. Additionally, we develop a "Chat with MARS" capability that enables interactive human-in-the-loop map generation and correction, supported by the auto-regressive nature of our end-to-end approach. We release our MAP-3M dataset and project demo page at (1) https://huggingface.co/datasets/bag-lab/MAP-3M and (2) https://huggingface.co/spaces/bag-lab/MARS, respectively.

Abstract: While foundation models and vision transformers (ViTs) have redefined visual representations, current active learning (AL) strategies for these backbones largely operate at the patch or region level, where annotation requirements remain high due to extensive pixel labeling. Extending ViT-based AL to the pixel-level, low-budget regime is uniquely challenging: with as little as one pixel query per image, a method must simultaneously identify the most informative locations and train a reliable decoder head from an extremely sparse signal. We introduce TEAL (Token-space Efficient Active Learning), the first pixel-level AL framework built around ViT representations. TEAL uses a frozen ViT backbone and performs diversity-based selection directly on its native token lattice, avoiding the artifacts introduced by interpolating token embeddings to dense pixel resolutions. Our framework applies a two-level MaxHerding strategy over multi-layer token descriptors to select representative candidates, followed by margin-based uncertainty refinement on a finer decoder grid. Across CamVid, Cityscapes, ADE20K, and Pascal Context, TEAL consistently outperforms previous baselines under extreme label scarcity, improving over the strongest baseline by up to +24.36 mIoU with a single labeled pixel (px) per image, and reaching up to 97% of fully supervised performance with just 10 px. To rigorously establish the source of the gains, we further show that the improvements persist when all baselines are equipped with the same DINOv3 backbone, demonstrating that coupling token-space selection with uncertainty refinement on the decoder grid makes ViT token geometry an effective foundation for extremely low-budget active segmentation.

</details>

Abstract: Existing long-document QA benchmarks rarely combine large-scale human-authored questions with detailed visual evidence annotations, limiting diagnosis of evidence localization and interpretation failures. We present VELD-QA, a large-scale benchmark for Visual Evidence grounding and question answering in Long Documents. It contains 15,000 fully human-authored question-answer pairs across 5,859 real-world PDFs totaling 372,445 pages, balancing chart, table, and complex-layout tasks. Human annotations identify supporting pages and evidence elements and describe cited visual content, enabling separate retrieval and interpretation evaluation. We evaluate nine vision-language models under four evidence settings and seven retrieval-augmented and agentic systems. Our analysis identifies bottlenecks in evidence localization and interpretation. Retrieval-based methods substantially underperform the oracle setting with ground-truth supporting pages; full-document access does not close the gap. Localization alone is insufficient: oracle accuracy peaks at 81.7%, highlighting persistent visual interpretation and reasoning challenges. Enriching inputs with our visual-element annotations narrows the performance gap by 17-24%, demonstrating value beyond evaluation and motivating data enrichment for multimodal document QA. Abstract: Geo-foundation models (GFMs) are typically evaluated by downstream task predictive performance, revealing little about which physical features they are sensitive to or use in prediction. This limits developers' ability to test hypotheses about model behaviour and users' ability to select models for new tasks. We propose Physics-Informed Concept Probing (PiCo), a post-hoc framework for explaining GFM predictions. PiCo identifies concept vectors in a GFM's embedding space by generating synthetic multispectral satellite observations from a 3D radiative transfer model coupled with a game engine. These vectors reveal physical sensitivities that inform domain-based hypotheses about downstream performance. Projecting embeddings onto these concept vectors produces low-dimensional representations for quantifying concept importance in making predictions. We evaluate PiCo on three GFMs with different input modalities using seven physical concepts covering forest leaf optics and canopy structure. In ground-cover estimation, RGB restriction reduces dry-matter sensitivity and disproportionately degrades predictions for dry vegetation and bare ground, supporting a hypothesis derived from sensitivity analysis. For hierarchical forest type and tree species classification, concept representations retain 70.0-93.8% of full-embedding accuracy. Models capture similar physical contrasts between classes, such as structural differences between broadleaf forest and cleared ground and leaf-water-content differences between oak and beech, yet differ in the strength of these contrasts and the concepts driving classification. PiCo thus provides a physical basis for hypothesis testing and model assessment, with potential applicability beyond Earth observation wherever forward models enable controlled simulation and pretrained embeddings are accessible for probing.

</details>

Abstract: Data selection reallocates training support across visual concepts, including objects, attributes, color, texture, and backgrounds. Different allocation strategies may produce varied downstream model behavior, especially for tail concepts supported by only a few samples. Discarding them can hurt test-time robustness, while overemphasizing samples lying on category boundaries may introduce unstable training signals. This work explicitly investigates this relationship across fine-grained, spurious-correlation, and general image classification, as well as multimodal pretraining. Specifically, we use sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to extract concept activations from individual images. During selection, we accumulate the dataset-level concept support for each added sample. Applying diminishing returns to this distribution down-weights well-represented concepts and shifts the focus to the distribution tail. We then incorporate local support to find samples that reliably strengthen under-supported concepts. Empirical results show consistent improvements over strong data selection baselines across all benchmarks. We also reveal the connection between concepts our method enhances and the induced behavioral changes, including improved robustness against out-of-distribution and spurious contexts. This work establishes concept support, beyond individual sample importance, as a useful principle for data-efficient and robust learning.

</details>

Abstract: Identifying individual dogs from barks in real-world videos remains challenging and largely unexplored. Solving this problem would enable long-term behavioral analysis, veterinary monitoring, and animal welfare monitoring by attributing vocalizations and behaviors to the correct individual. Real-world videos are challenging because multiple dogs may bark simultaneously, become occluded, or move in and out of view. Prior studies show that canine vocalizations and visual appearance contain discriminative identity information, but these modalities have largely been studied independently. We present a multimodal dog-fingerprinting framework combining visual identification, multiple-object tracking, and canine vocal fingerprinting to associate bark events with individual dogs while maintaining persistent profiles. The framework synchronizes bark events with tracked dogs and updates profiles using visual appearance and vocal characteristics, enabling identification despite occlusion, overlapping vocalizations, and off-screen barking. We also introduce a benchmark of 46 real-world videos containing 213 manually annotated bark events. Our framework correctly localizes the active visible dog in 61.83% of visible events at IoU $\geq 0.30$ and achieves a 91.33% F1 score for visible versus off-screen barking detection.

Abstract: Camera-to-robot pose estimation from a monocular image is significant for vision-based robot navigation and control. However, recovering 3D structural features from 2D observations remains a challenging task. Existing camera-to-robot pose estimation methods either rely on time-consuming iterative rendering or involve sophisticated 2D-3D registration which suffers from projection ambiguity, accumulated errors for distal joints and imbalanced learning for different joints. In this work, we propose RoPE-Flow, an end-to-end flow matching-based framework for camera-to-robot pose estimation. From the predicted 2D heatmaps, we propose a Keypoint-centered Feature Extractor to obtain positional features along with contextual representations from keypoints-centered multi-scale patches. Moreover, we introduce Graph-guided Condition Generator, which embeds a learnable adjacency map to generate condition for flow matching based on correlation among keypoints. Besides, we introduce Bone Length Loss and Ray-Depth Loss for topological consistency and scale stability, with joint-wise weighting to mitigate varied prediction difficulty across joints. The proposed method outperforms state-of-the-art methods for pose estimation across different types of robots with mutiple cameras. Besides, we establish a novel cross-scene benchmark to demonstrate the outstanding performance of RoPE-Flow on zero-shot cross-scene robot pose estimation. Meanwhile, animated visualizations are shown on the website: ropeflow.netlify.app

Abstract: We present RoboCloth, a dataset of 500 real cloth materials captured under flexible, calibrated camera and lighting configurations, where both are robotically controlled during acquisition. Each material is imaged in approximately 580 high-dynamic range frames spanning a wide view-light hemisphere; camera poses are refined via structure-from-motion and aligned to the robot frame, yielding per-material sparse surface geometry and structured per-point reflectance observations ready for neural material training. We demonstrate the value of this data through a two-stage neural reconstruction pipeline: Stage 1 trains a shared MLP decoder on our corpus to learn cloth material priors; Stage 2 then freezes the decoder and fits a dense 2048x2048 latent texture to each target material. Evaluating on held-out materials from our own dataset and on the independent UBO2014 benchmark, we show that a decoder trained on our captures outperforms decoders trained on existing measured reflectance datasets and analytic PBR fitting, transferring to unseen acquisition systems without modification. The dataset, calibration files, observation tensors, and train/test splits are released under CC BY 4.0 on the Hugging Face Hub.

Abstract: World and action modeling are closely related, yet their reciprocal benefits under unified causal training remain unclear. We introduce OneWM, a simple framework for unified causal training that treats actions as both conditions for world prediction and targets for policy learning within a shared video-action architecture. Using this framework, we investigate whether learning forward dynamics conditioned on specified actions improves action learning, and whether learning to generate actions in turn improves action-conditioned world modeling. We use LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboCasa365 as embodied testbeds to evaluate both policy learning and world modeling, complemented by real-world policy experiments. Learning the visual consequences of specified actions improves policy performance and generalization across changing scenes. In turn, action learning improves multi-view world-prediction fidelity under the same architecture. Together, these results answer both questions positively, supporting reciprocal benefits between world modeling and action generation under unified causal training.

Abstract: On-screen text can describe what a video shows, or it can frame what the video is supposed to mean. Current benchmarks treat both alike, testing whether a model can read the text rather than whether it should trust it. We study claim deference, multimodal large language models' tendency to let an on-screen claim override conflicting visual evidence. We introduce VisualTrust, a counterfactual benchmark with 5,063 cases across 14 claim-fact classes. Each case pairs a question with three versions of the same video: one with no claim, one with an accurate claim, and one with a misleading claim that alters a single grounded fact. The option the misleading claim supports is the trap answer. Across seven open-weight and four proprietary models, mean accuracy falls from 90.1% without a claim to 51.9% with a misleading claim. Among initially correct responses, 42.8% flip to the trap answer and only 0.6% become other errors. Strong no-claim accuracy does not ensure resistance. We also introduce DEAR (Decoupled Evidence Arbitration and Reading), a training-free agent for video question answering that separates evidence arbitration from reading. An independent check reports conflicting evidence, a selector weighs it without a fixed modality priority and blinds what it withholds, and the reader answers from the resulting video in a fresh context. On 500 videos across three proprietary models, DEAR raises accuracy under misleading claims from 47.2--64.0% to 81.8--85.2% and reduces trap-answer selection from 34.4--50.8% to 11.6--15.0%, while preserving no-claim accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Point-cloud streams are central to autonomous driving and robotics, yet existing segmentation networks process every frame from scratch, although consecutive frames largely overlap. We observe that the intermediate features of point-cloud transformers remain semantically consistent across frames for the same spatial region, especially in deep stages. Building on this, we present SpaceCache, a training-free feature cache that reuses these features across frames. Tokens of previously observed regions skip a sequence of transformer blocks and take their cached features, only the remaining tokens are computed, and the full token layout is restored for dense prediction. Lightweight cache management keeps reused features reliable by limiting each entry's age and evicting low-confidence entries. Unlike token merging, SpaceCache selects tokens at negligible cost and runs its cache operations once per encoder stage. On both outdoor and indoor benchmarks, such as nuScenes, Waymo, and ScanNet, with two transformer backbones of Utonia and LitePT-B, SpaceCache achieves up to 1.65x speedup with less than 1-point mIoU drop and consistently outperforms token-merging methods.

Abstract: Reliable image geometric estimation from noisy matching points is typically addressed using RANSAC, often combined with local optimization to refine inliers and model parameters. Abstract: Panoramic 3D reconstruction enables the holistic recovery of complete 360 $^\circ$ scene geometry, offering significant benefits for robotics and AR/VR applications. However, directly estimating geometry from ERP images is inherently challenged by spherical distortions. To reduce spherical projection distortions, a common strategy is to decompose panoramas into distortion-free perspective views, but independently processing these views often introduces cross-view geometric inconsistencies. Our approach is driven by two key innovations: First, we propose a panoramic-aware attention aggregator, featuring a streamlined attention architecture equipped with a novel Pano-Attention mechanism, which explicitly exploits the intrinsic spherical geometry of the panorama to guide cross-view interactions, thereby suppressing geometric inconsistencies. Furthermore, we formulate a hybrid supervision strategy to jointly enforce fine-grained local details in perspective space and global structural coherence in spherical space. Experiments demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms previous SOTA approaches in reconstruction accuracy, geometric consistency, and visual fidelity.

Abstract: Most vision-language models (VLMs) treat the vision encoder as a static feature extractor: it encodes an image once, independently of the question, and leaves subsequent reasoning to the language model. Yet different questions may require different visual operations and processing depths. We present AdaLoop, a framework for question-conditioned visual recurrence in VLMs. AdaLoop adapts visual computation to the question and reuses the encoder's transformations across multiple iterations, with the pretrained vision weights frozen. We also introduce VisRO, a procedural dataset and generation pipeline for training and evaluating vision-centric reasoning. On six VisRO tasks, AdaLoop outperforms standard fine-tuning and question-conditioned single-pass baselines with matched training data and optimizer updates. These gains hold across three VLM families on both in-distribution and out-of-distribution examples. Our findings suggest that pretrained vision encoders can contribute to reasoning through task-directed, iterative visual computation.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has brought strong gains to language model reasoning, but its improvements on visual tasks remain less consistent. One difficulty is that visual task failures can arise from different sources: the same benchmark may be limited by perception for one model and by reasoning for another. We introduce Perception and Reasoning Attribution (PARA), which measures the remaining perceptual and reasoning headroom of each model--benchmark pair by comparing how many current errors are recovered with more accurate visual information versus a stronger reasoner. Across 4 vision-language models and 17 multimodal benchmarks, PARA broadly agrees with the expected characteristics of existing benchmarks while revealing substantial model-specific variation. More importantly, it shows that RL gains become smaller as perceptual headroom increases, but larger when reasoning explains more of the recoverable errors, suggesting that current RL exploits reasoning headroom more effectively than perceptual headroom. Motivated by this gap, we analyze how existing RL objectives affect visual perception and find that reward- and token-level signals influence visual attention only indirectly. We therefore propose GRAD, a group-relative attention distillation objective that uses the best rollout in each group to directly guide the visual attention of weaker rollouts. GRAD consistently improves performance across two model scales and seven benchmarks, and further complements existing RL methods.

Abstract: Traditional keypoint descriptors, such as SIFT (Scale-Invariant Feature Transform) and ORB (Oriented FAST and Rotated BRIEF), are computed from local image patches. They may be unreliable in scenes with repetitive patterns or weak textures, where patches at different locations can look similar. We propose the Contextual Line-Assisted Descriptor (CLAD), a keypoint matching method that addresses this problem by using global geometric context from line correspondences. CLAD represents each keypoint with a vector whose elements indicate its proximity to matched lines. It can therefore use the keypoint's spatial relationships to the lines in the scene. We use HOME (Hough-space One-dimensional Matching of Extrema) to detect both clear and weak linear structures and to establish enough line-to-keypoint associations to represent this context. Evaluations on the TUM RGB-D SLAM dataset and outdoor video sequences show that CLAD provides geometrically consistent matches that complement those obtained with traditional local descriptors. In repetitive scenes, where local descriptors may not distinguish similar patches, CLAD uses global structure to improve matching reliability. The method is computationally lightweight and practical to implement.

</details>

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) receive image-generation supervision that exposes them to visual information not fully documented in text. Abstract: When a vision-language model is finetuned on class-imbalanced data, rare-class recall can fall well below the model's own zero-shot performance. In this regime, we uncover a surprising matched-prompt penalty whereby the standard choice of reusing the descriptive finetuning prompt at inference can yield lower rare-class recall than using a different descriptive prompt. We call this inference-time strategy cross-prompt inference. We evaluate diverse VLMs across six datasets and find that cross-prompt inference consistently recovers rare-class recall, with varying trade-offs in frequent-class recall. The effect reflects a train-eval prompt interaction rather than prompt quality alone, and does not arise with non-descriptive prompt changes. It is strongest when imbalance and visual confusability produce substantial rare-class suppression, and is weak or absent when such suppression does not occur. Using Tuning Contribution (TuCo), which decomposes a model's computation into pretrained and finetuning parts, we find that cross-prompt inference reduces reliance on finetuning-induced computation and shifts the model toward its pretrained component. Reducing that finetuning contribution also substantially reduces the cross-prompt advantage. These results show that rare-class knowledge suppressed by imbalanced finetuning can remain accessible under a different inference prompt.

</details>

Abstract: Real-to-sim conversion aims to turn a captured video of a real environment into an editable 3D scene while preserving the geometry and layout of what was observed. Existing methods often follow a one-pass, loosely coupled paradigm: each stage receives only limited feedback from previous stages, allowing errors to propagate without reliable correction and leaving the final scene inconsistent. We present GRASE, an agentic framework that closes this loop by treating the captured multi-view video observations as a standing reference throughout scene modeling. Instead of committing to intermediate estimates, GRASE revises its current hypothesis whenever rendered evidence disagrees with the capture, overcoming the fixed intermediate assumptions of conventional pipelines. Specifically, GRASE first fits the architectural shell and its openings from depth and multi-view cues. An agent then places generated assets in a shared coordinate frame while checking their spatial relationships. Finally, each asset's geometry and placement are refined iteratively to improve agreement across the observed views. By coupling these steps through feedback, GRASE localizes structural discrepancies and revises only the responsible components, while preserving cross-view consistency. The resulting 3D scenes are faithful to the input, consistent across views, and suitable for simulations. In our experiments, GRASE substantially outperforms other state-of-the-art methods, including a direct real-to-sim conversion using GPT6-Astra.

</details>

Abstract: Decision trees can make vision-language model (VLM) classification interpretable by replacing a single prediction with a sequence of root-to-leaf questions. However, the accuracy cost of this greedy, non-backtracking procedure remains unclear. We introduce \textbf{MYOPIA}, a benchmark comprising four LLM-generated, human-refined decision trees for GTSRB, MNIST, CIFAR-10, and MSCOCO, together with matched prompts, diagnostic protocols, and an evaluation harness. Across GPT-4o, Qwen-VL Max, and LLaMA-3.2 11B Vision, tree traversal underperforms zero-shot prompting in all 12 dataset--model pairs; paired bootstrap analyses consistently favor zero-shot. Our diagnostics characterize interacting failure modes associated with this gap. Models often recall tree-encoded class attributes more accurately than they ground the same attributes in images: the mean label--image accuracy gaps are 9.5, 13.8, and 12.6 points for GPT-4o, Qwen-VL Max, and LLaMA, respectively. Incorrect local decisions then irreversibly exclude the correct class, while answer extraction introduces additional model-dependent loss. A node-level audit of the four trees finds that split entropy is weakly but consistently associated with lower node accuracy, whereas depth, branching factor, and within-node grounding differences are less stable. \textbf{MYOPIA} therefore provides a framework for diagnosing hierarchical visual classification and motivates testing tree designs that prioritize perceptual answerability and recovery from early errors. Code is available at https://github.com/islammesabah/VLM_Classification.

Abstract: As vision-language models (VLMs) transition from frozen-encoder pipelines to native multimodal architectures, the visual channel increasingly serves as both a perceptual input and a carrier of rendered text that competes with user prompts. Current single-attack, pixel-centric evaluations fail to capture this complexity: they collapse distinct visual attack surfaces into a single scalar and ignore how architectural shifts alter vulnerability. Notably, we uncover a see-but-not-deceived signature where native VLMs attend heavily to typographic overlays but visually reject them, and we identify an objective mismatch between encoder-level and answer-level attacks in deep-fusion models. Evaluating 18 open-weight VLMs across a novel nativeness taxonomy, we demonstrate that legacy single-attack rankings (e.g., PGD) are unstable for native models. We demonstrate the pipeline's utility by evaluating two markerless registration strategies on clinical data spanning 11 vertebrae across 1,500 frames from three surgical sequences: the first such evaluation against an independent subsurface reference in a clinical setting. Abstract: As image generation models become increasingly capable of producing highly realistic content, traditional forensic techniques are struggling to keep up. Specifically, the study aims to determine whether these models detect fake images by recognising physical inconsistencies, or whether they rely on digital artefacts such as embedded watermarks.Six MLLMs: GPT-4o, GPT-5, GPT-5.2, Gemini 2.5, Gemini 3, and Seed 1.6, are benchmarked using a dataset of 400 images. Fifty images are authentic photographs, while the remaining 350 are generated using seven leading image models: Gemini 3, Gemini 2.5, Flux.2 Pro, GPT-Image-1, GPT-Image-1.5, Seedream 4.0, and Seedream 4.5. The evaluation is structured in three stages: single-image classification, pairwise comparison, and a focused "self-detection" experiment on Gemini. Several MLLMs achieved a high detection accuracy, with GPT-5.2 reaching a peak of 97.9% in the pairwise experiment, against specific generators and an overall mean pairwise accuracy of 82.5% by identifying structural or physical inconsistencies within images. However, results from the self-detection experiment indicate that the presence of invisible SynthID watermarks leads to a 10% reduction in Gemini's detection accuracy compared to the clean control group. One possible explanation is that SynthID embedding reduces some of the visible artefacts the models rely on for detection.These results imply that MLLMs can detect structural artefacts without relying on watermarks, but performance varies substantially across model generations. The findings from comparison C5 also suggest that SynthID embedding may suppress some of the structural artefacts used during detection.

Abstract: Multi-modal semantic segmentation benefits from complementary sensors, but this benefit is fragile when some modalities are degraded or missing at test time. Many existing methods rely on dedicated fusion modules or multi-branch cross-modal encoders, and the fusion often becomes unreliable when modalities are absent, leading to substantial performance drops. We propose \textbf{FuseAdapt}, a parameter-efficient framework that performs fusion within the adaptation space of a frozen vision foundation model (VFM), exploring whether VFM priors can serve as an effective shared backbone for multi-modal segmentation. Instead of attaching a standalone fusion network, FuseAdapt assigns each modality a lightweight adaptation branch and performs \emph{adaptation-space fusion} by keeping Q/V low-rank adaptation private to each modality and routing only FFN low-rank residual activations across modalities inside the frozen backbone. The pretrained VFM remains fixed while modality-specific corrections and explicit cross-modal interaction are learned through the adaptation paths. To handle missing modalities, we further introduce \textbf{Predictive Modality Modeling (PMM)}, a direct representation-level objective for cross-modal redundancy. During training, PMM masks a target modality and predicts its embedding from the visible ones, supervised by a teacher target computed under full-modality input. PMM is used only during training and adds no inference-time overhead. Across three multi-modal segmentation benchmarks (MCubeS, DELIVER, MUSES), FuseAdapt consistently improves both modality-complete and modality-incomplete segmentation with a small trainable budget, yielding up to +3.01 mIoU under full modalities and +12.56 mIoU under missing modalities over prior state-of-the-art. Project page: https://devinxzhang.github.io/FuseAdapt-Page/.

Abstract: We study how vision-language models (VLMs) can learn to think and actively explore in 3D space. Instead of providing multi-view images, videos, or 3D representations to the model, we preserve the native visual encoding of VLMs, i.e., a single image, one at a time, just like humans, and treat the 3D representation only as an environment with which the model can interact through actions (e.g., move left) and observations (rendered images). Each rollout begins from an initial first-person view and a visual question. The model can then iteratively reason, navigate, and reorient its viewpoint before committing to a final answer. Following an initial trajectory-supervised finetuning, we apply reinforcement learning (RL) with rewards driven primarily by final-answer correctness. Crucially, RL provides no direct supervision over which viewpoints the model should visit or which exploration actions it should take. Instead, the model must learn an effective exploration policy solely from the extent to which its interactions improve performance. Across six 3D benchmarks, our model ACT3D achieves an average accuracy of 53.2% that improves its base Qwen model by 22.0% and surpasses proprietary Gemini 3.6 Flash. ACT3D also outperforms existing open-source 3D methods while using 50-70% fewer input frames, and generalizes to unseen domains with an average improvement of 5.9% over its supervised initialization. Further analysis reveals adaptive, question-conditioned exploration. Performance gains over the base model increase from 0.6% on questions answerable from the initial view to 16.3% on those requiring extensive exploration. ACT3D searches for occluded targets, avoids unnecessary exploration, and acquires complementary viewpoints for spatial comparisons. These findings demonstrate that outcome-based RL enables VLMs not only to reason over visual evidence, but also to learn when and how to acquire it.

</details>

Abstract: Despite the remarkable generalization capabilities of Large Language Models, generating clinically accurate and structured radiology reports from medical images remains a significant challenge. Current end-to-end approaches function as black boxes, prone to factual hallucinations and generating ungrounded findings. This lack of reliability and failure to adhere to domain-specific structuring protocols, fundamentally limits their clinical utility. We present MedPAO-VL, a multimodal agent that shifts the paradigm from monolithic generation to explainable, iterative, protocol-compliant structured report generation. Unlike deterministic models, our plan-act-observe loop functions as a goal-oriented agent: it first perceives the Chest X-Ray through an explainable vision module designed to capture inherent diagnostic uncertainty, then iteratively refines the generated report by observing semantic discrepancies and self-correcting its generation for better alignment with visually detected clinical findings. Evaluation of our framework demonstrates that this agentic reasoning improves clinical correctness and completeness, achieving an expert-rated accuracy score of 3.5/5 and a structural score of 4.3/5. By prioritizing pathology specific explainability and iterative refinement over opaque end-to-end mapping, MedPAO-VL offers a transparent and clinically viable alternative for automated structured radiology report generation.

Abstract: Foundation models have shown significant potential across diverse fields and have been applied to medical imaging, but their robustness across patient populations remains unclear. We harmonize VinDr-CXR and PediCXR into a five-label classification task and evaluate six foundation models and two CNN baselines. All models are adapted using adult data only and tested in both the in-domain and pediatric domains using linear probing, partial fine-tuning, and full fine-tuning. The benchmark reveals a clear and consistent trend: all models suffer a substantial performance degradation when transferred from adult to pediatric data, with drops exceeding 10 percentage points. While Macro-AUROC values reach approximately 92% on the adult test set, performance decreases to around 78% on the pediatric domain. Another important finding is that increasing the depth of adaptation does not significantly improve cross-population generalization. Moreover, strong in-domain performance does not translate into better performance on pediatric data.

Abstract: Embodied spatial intelligence requires more than visual recognition: models must localize entities, reason about their relations, and express this knowledge in action-relevant forms. Yet joint training can conflate the knowledge needed at different stages because supervision is fragmented across incompatible geometric and semantic outputs. We introduce Anvil, a unified framework for data generation, training, and benchmarking for embodied spatial intelligence. (1) Anvil-Gen is a heterogeneous data flywheel that combines form-specific generation, cross-stage consistency verification, and failure-driven recollection across 11 Ground--Relation--Act forms. (2) Anvil-Ben provides scene-aligned supervision for learning diverse embodied spatial capabilities and scene-linked assessments that distinguish stage competence from error propagation. (3) Anvil-MOPD is a 9B student that consolidates heterogeneous spatial knowledge through distillation from specialist teachers. Semantic-first-fault routing targets teacher supervision to the student's earliest decisive failure, while stage normalization controls the loss scale across native outputs of different lengths. We construct 5.8M source-grounded records spanning 160K images, reserving 580K spatial records for held-out evaluation and using the remaining 5.22M records for training. On average across seven public benchmarks, Anvil-MOPD outperforms Qwen3.5-9B and RynnBrain-8B by 9.29 and 11.03 percentage points, respectively, while reaching 80.67% on VABench-Point. Controlled studies support targeted coverage, selective transfer, and feedback-guided repair as complementary framework components.

Abstract: Language-guided 3D affordance grounding aims to localize functional regions on 3D objects according to interaction intents. However, existing methods exhibit poor generalization to unseen objects. One challenge is the query-target gap: a semantic discrepancy between query features encoding interaction intents and target-region features, leading to inaccurate grounding. End-to-end training favors query representations tailored to seen objects, limiting their compatibility with shifted target-region features on unseen objects. To address this issue, we propose BridgeAfford, a framework that reduces the query-target semantic gap from two complementary perspectives. First, Interaction Region Alignment (IRA) promotes cross-object alignment among geometrically similar interaction regions during training. This enables structures on unseen objects that resemble previously observed ones to produce representations similar to those learned during training, facilitating target-region recognition. Second, Visual Evidence Aggregation (VEA) leverages a 2D affordance model to refine the attention distribution and guide the query toward the correct region, yielding more target-aware queries. Extensive experiments on the LASO and PIAD datasets demonstrate that BridgeAfford outperforms existing methods, particularly on unseen objects, highlighting its robust generalization ability.

</details>

Abstract: Semantic segmentation is critical for the reliable navigation and scene understanding of autonomous systems, yet its performance degrades severely in low-light environments due to noise, artifacts, colour distortion, and the loss of structural details. To address this challenge, this work introduces VesperMUSE, a unified multi-modal framework designed to achieve robust image semantic segmentation across diverse illumination conditions. The architecture addresses the light deficiency problem on multiple levels: proposing a novel physics-informed synthetic low-light adaptation algorithm, approaching Low-Light Image Enhancement (LLIE) through a thermal-guided paradigm, and finally aiming to recover the intrinsic scene details for semantic segmentation through a multi-modal fusion approach. Following enhancement, the framework dynamically integrates the primary RGB features with auxiliary thermal representations and surface normal maps highlighting geometric discontinuities and material boundaries. At the core of this integration is a highly scalable, modality-agnostic self-attention mechanism that extracts both global context and fine local features from an arbitrary number of auxiliary inputs. By combining and projecting these hierarchical multi-modal representations into a unified component, the attention module allows the network to selectively attend to relevant cross-modal cues without requiring any architectural modifications when different sensor configurations are used. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that VesperMUSE successfully recovers missing visual cues, providing a flexible, state-of-the-art (SOTA) solution for machine perception in extreme and unstructured real-world environments.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used to interpret religious, educational, and culturally sensitive visual content, yet their reliability on citation verification remains underexplored. We introduce Visual Sanad, a controlled benchmark for testing whether VLMs falsely accept incorrect Qur'anic verse attributions when the same quote-reference pair is rendered under different visual framings. The benchmark contains 2,400 rendered images built from 100 English Qur'an verses, four factual states per verse, and six visual conditions ranging from plain text to decorative and authority-framed layouts. All quote-reference pairs were source-checked and human-audited before rendering. Across 24 full model-prompt runs, we observe a sharp capability split. Proprietary API-served VLMs range from near-perfect citation verification to moderate false acceptance, whereas recent open-weight VLMs often show weak discrimination and default to VALID on most images. De-biased prompting can reduce false acceptance, but often by shifting the decision threshold toward INVALID rather than by producing strong citation discrimination. Visual Sanad therefore exposes a culturally grounded multimodal factuality failure: fluent VLMs may treat displayed religious citations as self-authenticating unless they have strong citation-verification ability.

</details>

Prompting helps unevenly: visual cues move accuracy by more than 10 points for some models but hurt others, and foregrounding Diwani often relocates the default rather than removing it. Arabic calligraphy recognition is therefore a compact, culturally grounded stress test for VLM robustness and for measuring which visual traditions become legible to automated systems.

Abstract: Streaming vision-language models (VLMs) must continuously integrate information from the past while remaining responsive to current observations and user queries. Existing memory mechanisms typically compress, retrieve, or cache historical observations and reintroduce them into the input context, forcing historical memory and current observations to compete for limited context. We propose MaLoW, a streaming memory framework that instead internalizes historical context into evolving lightweight parameter updates. MaLoW uses a recurrent HyperNetwork to incrementally consolidate incoming video observations into fixed-size LoRA weights, allowing historical information to be carried forward without revisiting the full video stream. In doing so, MaLoW represents history as weights, while keeping the current observation as input. To support training, we further construct MaLoW-Instruct, a large-scale streaming video dataset containing 381K question-answer pairs over 56K videos. We conduct extensive experiments to demonstrate that MaLoW achieves strong performance on OVO-Bench, StreamingBench, and StreamArena. On the backward split of OVO-Bench, MaLoW outperforms prior memory approaches by 3.9% to 11.8% across different video backbones while incurring only modest latency overhead. Beyond streaming video understanding, MaLoW also generalizes to vision-language-action models, improving the action success rate on Simpler WidowX by 9.3%.

Abstract: Recent work shows that depth recurrence can be retrofitted into pretrained language models. In this work, we adapt this paradigm for visual understanding, using depth recurrence to reduce the parameter footprint of a pretrained vision-language model (VLM). To this end, we prune layers from the language backbone of the VLM and loop over a subset of the remaining layers. At each step of the loop, a linear adapter merges the block's output with the token representations from the preceding layers and projects the fused representation back into the block. As the pruning impacts the original model performance, we propose a three-stage recovery training: first, we leverage a language-only pretraining to recover the performance of the language backbone, followed by an alignment of the vision-language projector, and a full fine-tuning of the model via visual instruction tuning. In the first and the last stage, we distill the outputs of the pretrained non-recurrent model into the recurrent model, which further improves the recovery. We evaluate the proposed approach on the TinyLLaVA-1.4B backbone without introducing any new vision-language data during retrofitting. Our experiments demonstrate that the retrofitted recurrent model outperforms the unpruned baseline trained with the same recovery data while storing 23.3% fewer language parameters. We further show that the proposed retrofitting recurrence outperforms the setup of converting a recurrent LM into a VLM on the same data. Additionally, our ablations show how design choices, such as the adapter between recurrences and a larger recurrent block, lead to the improved performance of our approach. Finally, we find that our proposed model gains on reasoning-oriented benchmarks, whereas its performance on basic visual understanding benchmarks remains on par with the baseline.

</details>

Abstract: Recent 3D world models increasingly use features from geometry foundation models (GFMs) as predictive world states, benefiting from their rich geometric priors. However, the view dependence of these features entangles scene dynamics with camera-induced variation, making the resulting state evolution harder to predict. We propose GeoState, a view-decoupled geometry representation for predictive 3D world modeling that separates scene-state evolution from camera-dependent observation. Specifically, a scene tokenizer aggregates multi-view GFM features into a view-decoupled scene state, which a geometry decoder queries using camera rays. An autoregressive world model then predicts future scene states, while camera motion is modeled separately. The predicted states can therefore be decoded under either predicted or externally specified cameras, while retaining a single context-derived scale throughout the rollout without per-frame scale alignment. Experiments show that GeoState outperforms prior 3D world models on Waymo, supports geometrically consistent cross-view querying, and generalizes zero-shot to KITTI and nuScenes. Abstract: In pathology, pattern recognition relies on a frame of reference built through extensive training and experience, which may still fall short when encountering rare cancer subtypes. Archives of previously diagnosed tumors often contain relevant precedents, yet identifying the most relevant cases remains challenging. Here we explore whether content-based retrieval with current pathology foundation models facilitates rare cancer diagnostics without any task-specific training. Pathologists can query a digitized archive using a region of interest (ROI) from a whole slide image (WSI) and retrieve morphologically similar cases, along with their ICD-O codes and corresponding WSIs. The ROI can be selected manually or automatically using a gated attention-based multiple instance learning model. We evaluate our approach by leave-one-case-out on an imbalanced dataset of 137 salivary gland tumor cases spanning 12 ICD-O morphology categories, comparing four pathology foundation models (Atlas2, Atlas2-B, Atlas2-S, and UNI2h). Top-1/3/5 retrieval was 62.5%/76.7%/81.3% for Atlas2, 50.2/66.6/72.9% for UNI2h, 52.3%/71.5%/77.6% for Atlas2-B and 54.4/65.2/73.7% for Atlas2-S. Requiring no additional training or manual annotation, this approach makes departmental archives searchable with pathology foundation models, providing a low-barrier route to case-based differential diagnosis and extending the individual frame of reference to the collective experience of the department.

Abstract: We introduce P$^{3}$-WAM, a unified world action model for egocentric dexterous manipulation that learns 3D perception, action-conditioned prediction, and goal-directed planning through three complementary modes: Perceive, which reconstructs articulated hand motion and camera trajectories from video; Predict, which generates visual futures under prescribed actions; and Plan, which jointly generates action trajectories and their anticipated visual consequences conditioned on an initial image, a language instruction, and an optional visual goal. We introduce FutureLift, a backbone-agnostic refiner that represents each multimodal proposal as a heterogeneous future graph, with spatially grounded temporal nodes connected to agent histories, map elements, alternative modes, and, for joint prediction, other agents future trajectories. Bidirectional temporal edges allow later evidence to revise earlier segments in a single pass, and the updated nodes predict residual coordinate and confidence corrections. Requiring only proposals, scores, and scene context, FutureLift provides a common refinement interface for structurally different forecasting backbones. On Argoverse2 and the Waymo Open Motion Dataset, it consistently improves frozen proposals and outperforms native and specialized refiners across marginal and joint forecasting. On the WOMD motion-prediction leaderboard, FutureLift ranks first in minADE (0.5049m) and minFDE (0.9942m) among the compared standard-input methods, without LiDAR or ensembling, while achieving a competitive 0.4874 Soft mAP.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) can elicit specialized visual capabilities in vision-language models (VLMs), yet jointly optimizing heterogeneous objectives often introduces cross-capability gradient conflicts. Training experts independently mitigates this interference, but direct reward optimization may entangle specialized knowledge with reward-hacking biases. This paper proposes Skill-Decoupled On-Policy Expert Distillation (SCOPED), which trains capability-specific experts and extracts explicit skills through reward-guided reflection. During on-policy distillation, these skills guide expert supervision while remaining unavailable to the unified student, encouraging it to internalize procedural knowledge without explicit skills at inference time. Across eight multimodal benchmarks, SCOPED consistently outperforms the backbone and mixed-capability RL, demonstrating the effectiveness of skill-decoupled on-policy expert distillation for integrating independently acquired capabilities. Further analyses reveal interference during joint capability optimization and suggest that skill augmentation promotes more consistent expert trajectories, greater attention to task-relevant visual evidence, and better preservation of specialized expertise in the unified student. All code and data are available at https://github.com/NEUIR/SCOPED.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) enables language models to improve text-to-image (T2I) generation by rewriting prompts for a frozen image generator. Existing methods typically apply a single signal derived from the image to individual subword tokens, although objects, counts, attributes, and relations form coherent semantic spans. We propose SCOPE, an objective based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) that aligns update weighting with this structure. The rewriter jointly generates an enhanced prompt and its semantic partition. SCOPE pools shifts in policy log probabilities within each span to derive a shared coefficient for its token losses while retaining a single advantage for each completion. It requires neither separate span rewards nor additional image renders. Across Qwen-Image, SD3, and FLUX.1-dev, SCOPE achieves average relative gains over vanilla GRPO of 9.48% on GeneVal and 7.62% on T2I-CompBench. Ablation studies show that both semantic boundaries and pooling within spans contribute to these gains, supporting semantic structure as a practical inductive bias for prompt engineering with RL.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence lets an agent perceive and act in the physical world, and many of its judgments must be made at interaction rates: which spatial relation holds, how far an object is, or where to place it. Vision-language models (VLMs) produce these answers by free-form text generation, paying for autoregressive decoding and answer parsing even though the output space is compact: one of a few options, a scalar, or a 2D point. In light of this, we introduce MJev-Spatial, a System 1 multimodal decision model that tackles all three types of spatial understanding tasks within a unified classification framework. Specifically, MJev-Spatial treats each candidate as a class and scores it using the backbone's pretrained query and key projections, without adding parameters. The candidates are the given options for choice tasks, or value bins and image regions refined through coarse-to-fine classification for numerical and point prediction, respectively. At inference, each classification decision takes a single forward pass and generates no answer tokens. To train on heterogeneous spatial data efficiently, we supervise all coarse-to-fine stages jointly with teacher forcing in one forward pass, and combine this with length-aware global batching and cross-GPU load balancing, raising training throughput by 30.8% over the original batching strategy. Built on Qwen3.5-4B and trained on 12.3M question-answer pairs converted from existing spatial datasets, MJev-Spatial achieves the highest overall score (65.64%) among eight tested models, ranks first on seven of twelve benchmarks, and outperforms its unmodified autoregressive base model on all twelve, while running 2.5 times faster than the fastest autoregressive baseline and 5.6 times faster than the base model.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning can align visual generators with reward signals beyond their pretraining objectives, but existing score-function methods typically introduce stochasticity by perturbing the reverse denoising trajectory, leading to costly rollout-based optimization. We instead ask whether stochastic exploration can be introduced inside the generator while preserving its original sampler. Routing Policy Optimization (RPO) answers this question by turning attention routing into a stochastic exploration action. At a few selected denoising levels, each query block keeps its own block and samples further key blocks in proportion to the generator's native attention mass, or keeps dense attention. The sampled route perturbs the velocity of that transition, and a Gaussian likelihood around the dense velocity scores the perturbation with a score-function objective. RPO further uses a controlled two-rollout estimator: two rollouts share the same prompt, initial latent, routed denoising levels, and dense anchor layers, differing only in their sampled attention routes. Their reward difference provides an advantage signal for the routed transitions. Because the likelihood differentiates the dense velocity itself, the learned updates remain effective after routing is removed. At inference, RPO restores dense attention and retains the original deterministic sampler. Experiments on text-to-image and text-to-video generation show that RPO improves generation quality while achieving nearly 4x higher training efficiency than Flow-GRPO-style methods. Please see our project page at https://velox-world.com/rpo for visual results.

</details>
